#308. Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out the New York Times

Some VIP Twitter users woke up on Saturday expecting to have lost their coveted blue verification check marks in a previously announced purge by Elon Musk. Instead, Twitter appeared to target a single account from a major publication Musk dislikes and changed the language on its site in a way that obscures why users are verified.

Twitter had said it would “begin winding down” blue checks granted under its old verification system — which emphasized protecting high-profile users at risk of impersonation — on April 1. In order to stay verified, Musk said, users would have to pay $8 per month to join the platform’s Twitter Blue subscription service, which has allowed accounts to pay for verification since December.

But one high-profile account did lose its blue check over the weekend: the main account for the New York Times, which had previously told CNN it would not pay for verification.

William Shatner is mad he may have to pay to keep his blue Twitter checkmark. Elon Musk says too bad

After an account that often engages with Musk posted a meme this weekend about the Times declining to pay for verification, Musk responded in a tweet saying, “Oh ok, we’ll take it off then.” Musk then lashed out at the Times — just the latest instance of the billionaire slamming journalists or media outlets — in a series of tweets that claimed the outlet’s coverage is boring and “propaganda.”

The weekend moves are just the latest example of Twitter creating confusion and whiplash for users over feature changes — and in this case, not just any users, but many of the most high-profile accounts that have long been a key selling point for the platform. It also highlights how Musk often appears to guide decisions about the platform more by whims than by policy.

Although the New York Times’ main account lost its blue check, its other accounts, such as those for its arts, travel and books content, remained verified. (It’s not clear why the New York Times doesn’t have a gold “organizations” check mark, like accounts for other news outlets, including the Associated Press and the Washington Post.) After its blue check was removed, a spokesperson for the New York Times reiterated to CNN that it does not plan to pay for verification.

Twitter, which laid off most of its public relations staff last fall, did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a separate puzzling move, Twitter’s blue bird logo at the top of the site was replaced on Monday with doge, the meme representing the cryptocurrency dogecoin, which Musk has promoted. The price of dogecoin shot up 20% on Monday.

Musk has been threatening to take away “legacy” blue check marks from users verified under Twitter’s old system since shortly after he bought Twitter last fall.

In early November, Twitter launched the option for people paying for its Twitter Blue subscription service to receive blue checks. The program was quickly put on pause after being plagued by a wave of celebrity and corporate impersonators, and was relaunched in December.

Twitter also rolled out a color-coded verification system with differently colored marks for companies and government entities, but Musk continued to say that individual users would eventually have to pay for blue checks.

In the days leading up to the blue check purge that wasn’t, prominent users such as actor William Shatner and anti-bullying activist Monica Lewinksy pushed back against the idea that, as power users that draw attention to the site, they should have to pay for a feature that keeps them safe from impersonation.

By muddying the reason accounts are verified, the new label could risk making it easier for people to scam or impersonate high-profile users. Experts in inauthentic behavior have also said it’s not clear that reserving verification for paid users will reduce the number of bots on the site, an issue Musk has raised on and off over the past year.

Musk, for his part, has previously presented changes to Twitter’s verification system as a way of “treating everyone equally.”

“There shouldn’t be a different standard for celebrities,” he said in a tweet last week. The paid feature could also drive revenue, which could help Musk, who is on the hook for significant debt after buying Twitter for $44 billion.

Musk last week also said that starting on April 15, only verified accounts would be recommended in users’ “For You” feeds alongside the accounts they follow.

–CNN’s Oliver Darcy contributed to this report.

#307. The pros and cons of using OnePlus smartwatches

OnePlus watches offer great battery life for the money, but they leave out some features rivals have.

Though they have their dedicated fans, OnePlus devices have always been a bit niche. Just like the company's phones, OnePlus smartwatches stand toe to toe with competitors' in terms of performance, but remain far from the most popular on the market. With OnePlus' announcement that it will no longer be selling its devices in the US or Europe, now might be a strange time to start considering a new purchase, but it's not out of the question.

On the official OnePlus store, it's currently impossible to buy a new smartwatch in the US, but other options are available. Online retailers like Amazon still have stock of the entire smartwatch line for now. So the question remains: what do you gain or lose by choosing OnePlus smartwatches over any other brand, especially when you might not be able to find one if you wait too long?

Battery life has always been one of the biggest advantages of OnePlus devices, and its smartwatches are no different. The OnePlus Watch 4 boasts five days of use on a single charge in smart mode and up to two weeks in power saver mode.

OnePlus smartwatches also charge incredibly fast. Performance tests showed that a OnePlus Watch 3 reached up to 80% charge in 30 minutes. By comparison, a Apple Watch Series 10 reached 50% and a Samsung Galaxy Watch Ultra only 36%. That means there's almost no risk of your OnePlus Watch battery ever running dry if you're paying the slightest bit of attention.

The other big advantage for OnePlus is that its smartwatches are more affordable than comparable competitors without sacrificing quality. Despite being a bit cheaper, OnePlus watches are well made with large, bright AMOLED screens. Their onboard software, called OHealth, is generally considered serviceable but not great, but the full suite of Google apps is available on OnePlus watches as well.

The build quality of OnePlus watches is also a point in their favor. OnePlus watches are solid and durable, which matters a lot when they're meant to be worn during physical activity. They're designed to look more like traditional watches, which generally makes them more aesthetically appealing than other smartwatches, but that can also be a matter of taste.

On the other hand, OnePlus watches lag behind on some major features. The big one is cellular support: OnePlus watches have none. That means that you need to pair one with a phone to make it useful, and since they use Wear OS, OnePlus watches are only compatible with Android phones. Since OnePlus didn't get the required FDA approval, US models lack ECG trackers; older OnePlus Watch 2 models also lack fall detection.

Build quality can also be a double-edged sword. OnePlus watches are chunky devices, and despite their good looks, that can make them stand out a bit too much if you prefer something sleeker. Their size also makes them hard to wear if you have slimmer wrists, and they're heavier than the competition, which could make them a pain over long periods.

OnePlus has promised to continue supporting existing devices with software updates, but it's unclear how easy support will be to reach. Smartphone users will likely feel the impact sooner than smartwatch owners since watches rely less on software updates, but it's something to consider before getting too used to the feel of OnePlus watches.

#306. Meta is expanding AI age detection for Instagram teen accounts

The company will increase its use of the tech on Facebook and Threads too.

Meta is expanding its use of AI age-estimation systems globally to place more Instagram users around the into teen accounts. The company has been using this tech in the US, European Union and Brazil to estimate a user's age. If the systems determine the person is aged between 13 and 17, they'll be funneled into a teen account, even if they used an adult date of birth while signing up.

Teen accounts are more restrictive than standard ones as they have age-appropriate protections. They prevent strangers from messaging teens, make profiles and posts private by default, employ daily app time limits and have parental controls. Meta will soon start sending parents in the US a notification on Instagram to explain how teens are placed into these accounts.

Meta is continuing to expand its use of AI age-estimation tech on Facebook as well. The company plans to bring it to Threads later this year. In Australia, it's starting to use Apple's age-range system as an extra age-detection signal (Meta has long been pushing for legislation that would make app stores responsible for verifying users' ages).

In August, the company reached an agreement (under which it may have to pay as much as $18 billion) with 52 attorneys general to settle a lawsuit that alleged harms to young users of its apps. As part of that settlement, the company said it's "investing in even stronger technology to proactively catch accounts that may belong to children under 13" and to detect accounts belonging to 13- to 17-year-olds.

Update, November 9, 2026, 2:12PM ET: A previous version of this story indicated that Meta is using bone structure analysis of photos, as well as mentions of things like school grades or birthday celebrations to estimate a person's age. The company clarified to Engadget that these measures are used to detect and remove people aged under 13 from its platforms.

#305. SpaceX now fundamentally an "infrastructure company," senior official says

“Grok analyzing the same video is 99.9 percent perfect.”

For a long time, SpaceX has been synonymous with the development of newer, larger, and ever-more-reusable rockets. But that began to change over the last decade with the introduction of Starlink, and the change has accelerated during the last 18 months as the company has pivoted to data and artificial intelligence.

At last week’s UP.Summit conference in Florida, the vice president of launch at SpaceX, Kiko Dontchev, offered some of the most revealing comments to date about the company’s changing focus, how it is embracing AI, and why the Falcon 9 rocket—which has dominated global launch for a decade and remains the world’s preeminent launch vehicle—is firmly on the road to retirement.

“In the last couple years, when I talk about SpaceX, yes, we are a rocket company, but fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point,” Dontchev said. “We’re building infrastructure on the ground and low Earth orbit, soon on the Moon, and obviously eventually Mars.”

Earlier this year, SpaceX acquired one of founder Elon Musk’s other companies, xAI. SpaceX engineers were already using xAI’s tools, Dontchev said, but in recent months, employees have really started to figure out how artificial intelligence can speed up their work.

“What’s happened at SpaceX is we’ve really gone through a huge acceleration of adoption,” Dontchev said. “Even just a year ago, we weren’t adopting it that quickly, but really starting this year, we started to adopt it extremely rapidly. And the acceleration we have seen in our ability to do engineering is unlike anything I’ve ever seen at the company in my 16 years.”

Tasks that would take SpaceX employees hours to days now take seconds to minutes, he said. And it’s not just the engineers. Dontchev said the work of identifying foreign object debris, or FOD, in aerospace parts has been largely automated using the AI assistant Grok. This debris can cause catastrophic damage to engines and other spacecraft components.

“We’ve automated being able to detect FOD,” he said. “We’ll have an inspector go in and borescope a tube, and we find that humans are about 94 percent perfect (at) finding a piece of it. Grok analyzing the same video is 99.9 percent perfect.”

SpaceX has also automated visual inspections of other hardware it produces, such as Starlink customer terminals, due to similar gains in accuracy. Dontchev said AI is now being used at every level of the company’s operations.

“We’ve truly started to integrate it to where every single person, including the technicians and the technician leaders, are starting to use it in their day-to-day, and it’s completely leveled the playing field and accelerated people’s growth,” he said.

Since SpaceX’s founding in 2002, a community of spaceflight enthusiasts gravitated to the company because of Musk’s vision for humanity becoming a multiplanetary species, beginning with the settlement of Mars. But concern has grown in this group that SpaceX is drifting away from that vision. Musk has recently made a hard pivot toward the deployment of orbital data centers, envisioning hundreds of thousands of solar-powered satellites providing computing services to users on Earth. Musk has largely stopped talking about sending humans to Mars, except in passing.

Dontchev insisted that human settlement and Mars remain part of the core of SpaceX’s aspirations.

“We have come to understand that this tool is critical to us accelerating our pace of innovation,” he said of artificial intelligence. “Without AI, I think we still would have gotten to the Moon. I think we still get to Mars with SI. We’re going to get there so much faster.”

Dontchev arrived at SpaceX just before the company flew its first Falcon 9 rocket in mid-2010. But because the company was never able to solve second-stage reuse, SpaceX needs to move on, he said.

“Falcon 9, I love that rocket to heart,” he said. “I’ve launched many of them, all of them, in fact. But it’s still not fully reusable. We still throw that second stage away every single flight. Starship—we’re going to catch both the first stage and the second stage.”

For 100 launches a year, it is possible to fly a partially reusable rocket, he said. But for a company that aspires to launch thousands of rockets a year, and eventually maybe even 10,000, the cost and time lost in manufacturing a new second stage for every launch is simply not sustainable.

It is also significant that Dontchev spoke less about Starship’s payload capacity and more about its productivity per launch. The first orbital Starship launch, in late September, carried larger Starlink V3 satellites. And in the aggregate, this single mission carried 10 times the bandwidth of the Starlink payload launched by a Falcon 9.

Flying Starship from Florida, he said, will further improve capacity compared to Texas launches. Launching from Florida offers more favorable trajectories to certain orbits, potentially allowing Starship to carry more satellites without expending propellant on dogleg maneuvers. Dontchev said a single Florida Starship launch will be able to carry up to 20 times the bandwidth of a single Falcon 9.

To that end, the pad infrastructure for Starship’s first launch tower at Kennedy Space Center’s Launch Complex 39A is nearly finished. The company is also developing two additional pads in Florida.

“Pad 39A is effectively complete,” he said. “We’re going to transport boosters and ships over barge here to the Cape. We’re hoping to actually do the first transports this year in December, and then maybe even fly by the end of the year.”

Starship is clearly being optimized to serve SpaceX’s launch needs. But what about commercial launch customers that have relied on Falcon 9 rockets over the last decade?

During his remarks, Dontchev did not address widespread concerns in the US commercial space industry and beyond that SpaceX has not moved to replace the Falcon 9’s lost capacity with Starship. For now, multiple sources have indicated, SpaceX is holding Starship launches in the coming years for its own needs, and potentially those of NASA and other government customers.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#303. A transitioning Congress finds itself in a pickle on AI data centers

As Congress returns to Washington this week, the massive power demands of the AI boom have forced a reckoning over data center infrastructure.

The Senate is preparing for a pivotal procedural vote on legislation that would speed approvals for energy projects while requiring large data centers to shoulder more of the costs they impose on the power grid. The bipartisan bill would add much-needed regulations on data centers, but, as some politicians believe, may also encourage more data center construction.

In the sweeping and history-making midterm elections last week, voters didn't just elect a new generation of Democratic lawmakers. They also sent a pretty unmistakable message to Big Tech, particularly the artificial intelligence industry: Enough with the data centers already. Or, more bluntly, "get out of town."

Across the country, local communities are pushing to stop, restrict, or impose conditions on data center development. The election results reinforced that message, making clear that the AI gold rush is facing growing resistance.

For the last two years, Silicon Valley has busied itself building more, building bigger and doing it ASAP. If a tech giant wanted to drop a warehouse-sized data center into a community, local governments often offered tax breaks and other incentives, then told residents it was harmless and would mean more jobs.

Democrats now find themselves in a pickle. Many campaigned on restricting data center development, responding to voters concerned about electricity costs, water consumption, and the impact these enormous facilities have on their communities.

Now, the aforementioned pending legislation forces a stark about-face — since, as Reuters reports, some Democrats believe provisions in the proposed bill could encourage the creation of more data centers. If that comes to pass, incoming lawmakers would need to explain why those campaign promises to halt data center construction no longer apply.

There's a risk, too, in the new Democratic Congress pushing too forcefully for restrictions on AI, specifically on data centers. My well-seasoned brain believes AI companies will respond to restrictions not simply by complaining about the cost of doing business, but by slowing down the rollout of new technology.

Some of that slowdown will be unavoidable. Without enough data centers, electricity, and computing capacity, companies cannot expand indefinitely. But some of it could be strategic.

I suspect the message from Silicon Valley will be slightly PR-ish: If you don't want us building data centers, don't expect your ChatGPT, Claude, or Siri to keep getting faster, smarter, and more capable. That "hurry up and now" diagnosis for your sore knee might take a little longer.

Picture AI as something other than a magical cloud floating above our heads. Every time you ask an AI assistant to write an email, generate an image, or summarize a document, powerful chips in actual buildings are cranking away, consuming tremendous amounts of electricity and water and pressuring local power grids.

And all that power costs mega cash. Utility rates, grid upgrades, and higher electricity bills have turned an abstract technology debate into something much more personal. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

I don't blame communities for pushing back. Why should residents subsidize a multibillion-dollar technology company's expansion while wondering whether their electric bills will keep climbing? They're already fed up with rising gas and grocery prices and the cost of a new home.

But there's another side to this costly coin that Democrats shouldn't ignore. Data centers can bring economic benefits, too, provided technology companies invest in the communities that host them.

While data centers rarely employ local, low-skill workers directly, AI companies can bridge this gap by funding local infrastructure projects that immediately put the existing workforce to work. Furthermore, the resulting influx of permanent, high-salaried technicians injects new tax revenue into the community, triggering a powerful economic ripple effect for local restaurants and shops.

Communities shouldn't have to choose between protecting residents and benefiting from technological investment. Companies that want to build in these towns should contribute meaningfully to their economic well-being.

Government intervention, even when well-intentioned, can also produce unintended consequences that ultimately hurt consumers.

Just look at Europe. In 2024, Apple said it would hold back Apple Intelligence and other new features from EU customers, citing "regulatory uncertainties" tied to the Digital Markets Act. Regulators were trying to rein in Big Tech and protect competition. Whether you buy Apple's explanation or see it as a pressure tactic, European customers were left waiting while others got the shiny new toys.

This doesn't mean all regulation is bad. It's a reminder that lawmakers cannot promise to put a tight leash on an industry without potentially creating bottlenecks in the products and services it provides. That's precisely the dilemma Democrats now face.

If they make it too difficult or expensive to build data centers, AI companies will have fewer places to house the computing power necessary to expand their products. New features could take longer to arrive. More sophisticated applications could cost more.

And I wouldn't be surprised if the industry makes sure consumers understand exactly who's responsible. They'll be smug about it: "OK, you wanted restrictions. You got them. Now don't lose it when your AI assistant takes longer to answer. Or, we might have you paying a monthly fee."

It's a convenient argument for Big Tech, shifting attention away from an industry that hasn't always sought communities' permission or given much thought to ordinary consumers.

That's why Democrats need to be thoughtful about what comes next. Protecting communities, keeping electricity affordable, and demanding that tech companies pay their fair share are entirely reasonable goals. But a political victory over data centers could become a consumer defeat if lawmakers don't consider the consequences.

#302. Think twice before using a webcam cover on your MacBook

The reasoning behind this hasn't changed much since our 2016 discussion on whether covering a laptop's camera is a wise move or paranoia. Yes, covering the lens can protect you from being spied on, but that small piece of plastic can also end up damaging your display.

This isn't our assumption — it comes from a recommendation by Apple. The company claims that closing the lid of a MacBook with a camera cover installed can damage the screen, since the gap between the display and the keyboard was designed with millimeter precision to ensure the lid closes properly. Anything you add between these two can put extra pressure on the components, resulting in cracks in the glass and display. Apple also notes that residue left from attaching and removing webcam covers can prevent other features that use the camera sensors, like automatic brightness, from working properly.

When you close the lid on your MacBook (whether it's a Neo, Air or Pro) the screen rests on top of the keyboard and palm rest. There's no room for anything else — the glass can even occasionally rub against the keyboard, leaving small imprint marks on the glass. This usually happens when you close the lid a little too hard or put pressure on it, like when your Mac is in a backpack with other items on top of it.

Apple places a thin rubber strip around the lid to better distribute pressure when the MacBook is closed. However, when you put something like a webcam cover on it, the pressure from the lid ends up concentrating on that small spot.

Although there's no guarantee that something bad will happen, this difference in pressure can result in permanent marks, cracks or even the display failing to turn on at all. The latest MacBooks make this even more complicated, since the camera is located in the notch area at the top of the screen. The bezels around this are so thin that there's almost no frame left to attach a webcam cover.

If you really have to cover the webcam on your MacBook, Apple recommends using covers that are as thin as a sheet of paper (about 0.1mm). Anything thicker than that could damage your MacBook's screen. It's also worth noting that damage caused by these webcam covers isn't covered under warranty, so you'll have to pay for the repair if anything happens.

Thankfully, most people don't need a physical cover for their webcam. Almost every MacBook has a green indicator next to the camera that lights up whenever it's in use. This light is inextricably tied to the webcam, so the camera cannot run without also triggering the light. For the MacBook Neo, which doesn't have a built-in LED indicator, macOS will display a green dot at the top-right that overlays everything else on the screen. Also, apps are required to ask for permission to use the camera on your Mac. If you see that light when you're not on a call or taking a photo, something might be wrong.

If your workplace requires you to have a webcam cover — or if you still want to use one — be sure to follow Apple's recommendations. It's also a good idea to remove the cover before closing your MacBook's lid. Our list of great accessories to buy for your MacBook includes a super-thin webcam cover.

#301. This boiled bag of offal is banned in the US. In Scotland it's a fine-dining treat

Anthony Bourdain loved haggis. But even the late, great American chef, writer and television host recognized that Scotland’s national dish, with its “sinister sheep parts” wrapped in a shroud of mystery and half-invented history, could be a hard sell.

“Don’t let them tell you otherwise, that’s really one of life’s great pleasures,” Bourdain said on one of his gastro-curious pilgrimages to Glasgow. “There is no more unfairly reviled food on Earth than the haggis.”

A mash-up of diced lung, liver and heart mixed with oatmeal, beef suet, onion and assorted spices, haggis was traditionally made by stuffing these raw ingredients into the stomach of a recently slain sheep and boiling the lot to a state of palatability.

Instagrammable is not the word that immediately comes to mind. In our 21st-century world, where “clean” eating and processed pap overlap, haggis can seem like an “Outlander”-style outlier from another age.

Yet, by some alchemy, once cooked to its required “warm-reekin’ (steaming)” state, it adds up to much more than the sum of its modest parts. It’s offaly charm has kept nose-to-tail eating alive among a younger generation of Scots that has largely turned its back on the tripe, liver and kidneys their predecessors enjoyed (or endured).

Carefully prepared, haggis tastes both oaty and meaty; it is dark and crumbly, a little crispy at the edges but still moist; earthy but also savory and spicy; deep-tasting and profoundly warming, the perfect foil for its traditional garnish of floury mashed potatoes and orange bashed turnip.

“It’s like a cuddle for the stomach,” says Nicola Turner, a 35-year-old office administrator from Helensburgh, a town on western Scotland’s Firth of Clyde.

For children of the 1960s and ’70s, like crime novelist Ian Rankin, haggis meals were a choice between the classic meat-and-two-veg plate and the battered and deep-fried, chip-shop iteration loved by both his friend Bourdain and his quintessentially Scottish detective character, Inspector John Rebus.

Now myriad other treatments have blossomed.

“I’m pretty sure the first time I dined with AB in Edinburgh we had haggis in filo pastry with a jam-style – maybe blackcurrant – sauce,” Rankin recalled. “He was a big fan of haggis and of chip shops. Rebus will have enjoyed the occasional haggis supper from his local chip shop. He was definitely a fan, as am I.”

“It is all about the spicing and the texture,” says the Scottish food writer, novelist and cook Sue Lawrence, a champion of haggis’ adaptability for use in other dishes. “If you didn’t know what was in it, you wouldn’t think ‘oh that tastes of liver or whatever.’ It is all nicely chopped up and the oatmeal gives it a lovely texture. It could easily be a nice, big mince dish.”

These US restaurants and chefs are James Beard Award finalists

Lawrence uses haggis as an alternative to beef and pork ragù in lasagna and in her pastilla, a version of the North African dish in which a hand-made haggis from the Isle of Mull substitutes for the traditional poultry or seafood filling. The filo pastry savory is flavored with the spice blend ras el hanout, apricots, chile, orange zest and almonds before being sprinkled with cinnamon and icing sugar.

Such cultural crossovers serve as a reminder that haggis could easily be a dish with nothing specifically Scottish about it at all. Records of similar quick and portable preparations of the fast-perishing innards of sheep and other animals date back to ancient Rome and Greece.

Haggis-like combinations of offal and grains are part of the culinary history of several countries. Spain has chireta, Romania drob and Sweden polsa, while chaudin, or ponce, is a rice and meat-stuffed pig stomach that is a staple of Cajun cooking.

In neighboring England, recipes for “hagese,” “hagws of a schepe,” “haggas” or “haggus” pop up in recipe books published between the 15th and 17th centuries, probably preceding written records north of the border.

Etymological evidence points to the term “haggis” having its roots in Old Norse, suggesting an early version of an oat-and-offal sausage might have arrived in Britain and Ireland on a Viking longboat.

But ever since it was first optioned by the poet Robert Burns in the late 1700s, the haggis backstory has been monopolized by Scotland and the Scots, sometimes mischievously.

It is, according to the kind of lore that Burns engendered, the dish a doughty Highlander would carry with him as he drove cattle through the glens to the markets of the central belt or the perfect picnic for a whisky smuggler plying his illicit trade by moonlight.

From such romantic notions it was a short step to turning the haggis into a wee wild beastie, one with longer legs on one side that was thus condemned to run round and round whichever hill it lived on. In 2003, a poll of American tourists in Scotland found that one in three of them believed they might encounter such a confused creature on a Caledonian vacation.

Bourdain, a native New Yorker, may have qualified as haggis’ biggest admirer since Burns, but his compatriots at the US Department of Agriculture remain unconverted to offal-filled paunch. Haggis imports into the United States were prohibited in 1971 as part of a ban on the consumption of all livestock lungs. Authentic versions of old school haggis remain culinary contraband in the US, as hard to lay your hands on as Cuban cigars.

Across the rest of the world, it’s a different story. According to leading producer Simon Howie, haggis is more widely appreciated and consumed now than it has been since Burns improvised his “Address to a Haggis” for the entertainment of well-to-do Edinburgh acquaintances.

Firmly tongue-in-cheek, the poem lauds the “Great chieftain o’ the pudding race,” as exactly the kind of unpretentious, hearty fare required to nourish a nation of braveheart warriors.

In comparison to the enfeebling foreign muck enjoyed by the capital’s claret-quaffing elites of the time – the olio, fricassée or ragoût that would “sicken a sow” – Burns urges his readers to wonder at the magical impact of haggis on his fellow sons of Scotland’s soil.

As the English translation of the original Scots language version puts it:

These days synthetic casings have largely replaced stomach but ovine and porcine innards remain at the core of most of the haggis produced in its homeland, said Howie, who estimates that his company Simon Howie Butchers, accounts for around 60% of the roughly two million haggises produced every year.

For Howie, versatility, value for money and convenience explain why this staple of the Scottish larder is thriving. Typically haggis retails in Scotland, which accounts for half of global consumption by volume, for around £6, or $7.70 per kilogram ($3.36/pound). That’s around half the price of less expensive cuts of beef or a third of the price of Scotch lamb while enjoying a fairly similar nutritional and calorific profile.

“You can give your kids a meal that is not full of things you don’t want to feed them – for a few pounds you can feed three strapping lads,” Howie said.

“From a kitchen perspective, it is very simple because when it leaves our factory it is already cooked. So when you or a restaurant owner gets it into the kitchen all you have to do is heat it up to be piping hot. It couldn’t be more basic: a student with no cooking skills or a Michelin-starred chef do exactly the same thing to put it out on the plate.”

Its texture means haggis can also be usefully deployed in fine dining alongside leaner meat like venison or as a stuffing for poultry and game birds. Its spicy intensity means it is also finding uses in canapés and as a crouton-borne garnish for soups.

Buoyant sales are also underpinned by the increasing consumption of haggis in forms inspired by Scotland’s ethnic minorities.

Glasgow’s Sikh community pioneered haggis pakora in the 1990s and samosas, spring rolls and quesadillas have followed in its wake, often using a vegetarian version of the protein in which the offal is replaced by a mix of vegetables, pulses and mushrooms.

Such dishes are more than culinary twists. They are badges of belonging, and an indication that, two centuries after Burns grabbed it for the nation, haggis is as intimately entwined with Scots identity as ever.

Just ask Ross O’Cinneide, a promising 14-year-old fly-half in the junior section of Stirling County rugby club.

“Most of my friends and I like haggis,” he says. “Mum makes it for us sometimes after rugby and it’s got a very nice warming feeling. And it’s nice because it’s purely Scottish.”

Scan the QR code to download the CNN app from the Apple Store.

#299. Mediums new app tries to save the platform from irrelevance

A personal essay, a tutorial, or an argument that's been knocking around in someone's head for weeks can all find a home on Medium. Now the platform wants to be part of what happens before those pieces are ready to publish — when a writer is still sorting through notes or sending an early draft to a friend.

On Nov. 9, Medium launched TK, a free writing and note-taking app for people with a Medium account. TK has an AI assistant built into the editor that can answer questions or give feedback as writers draft. It also lets writers share work in small groups where others can leave comments, swap notes, or contribute writing of their own.

It's goal, according to CEO of Medium Tony Stubblebine, is to "craft writing that compounds into deep thinking, which creates influence across your entire world. What shape is that influence? We’re still figuring that out, but we know it starts with humans writing more."

"We’ve designed TK for three goals: Easy to write. Easy to think. Easy to feel like your writing matters," he added.

The launch comes as the internet has changed. Medium relies on Google to send readers to its articles (44 percent of their traffic, specifically), and Stubblebine said those referrals declined "When Google started taking away all the traffic." He warned the company in an interview with The Verge that "this hurts us, but it also hurts you." Though Medium is profitable since 2024, iterating must be done to compete with both other publishing platforms (like Substack) and social media.

Stubblebine's response is to give writers a way to reach readers without relying on Google. With TK, they can share drafts directly with people in private groups. 'We’re just going to take everything underground," he told The Verge. Beyond Medium, in an analysis of March 2025 browsing data, Pew Research Center found that users clicked a traditional search result on 8 percent of Google visits with an AI summary, compared with 15 percent of visits without one.

For those who don't see the letters everyday (like me), "TK" is used by writers and editors to mark something in their work that's still to come, like a date that needs checking or a quote that needs dropping in. In Medium’s app, typing @TK calls up an assistant that can answer a quick question or find a paragraph the writer saved the day before. Medium says the assistant can also give headline and editorial feedback, compare drafts with Medium publications' style guides, and pull related notes into summaries that link back to the originals. Writers can import their Medium archives or bring over notes from Bear and Obsidian. An optional Claude integration lets an outside agent work with the notebook, too.

The human version of that feedback happens in what TK calls Libraries. A Library can be a space for a few friends to share work in progress, and Medium is also opening groups for journaling, getting feedback on drafts, and working toward a self-published book. A draft can stay private, go to a handful of readers, or eventually make its way to Medium's publishing platform. According to Stubblebine, "TK is the gym and Medium is the stage."

That division makes for an awkward question: The company wants people to do more of their own writing while putting AI a few keystrokes away from every unfinished sentence. Looking up a date is one thing, but asking AI for feedback on a paragraph can get a lot closer to outsourcing writing abilities. Other publishing platforms are finding uses for AI, too. Substack can turn a podcast recording into an editable transcript or suggest short clips from a video for creators to share on Notes or Instagram. And as Mashable reported in July, Substack also gives readers "Scan for AI text" option. The tool, powered by Pangram, estimates how much of a published post was written by a human, generated by AI, or written with AI assistance.

The app comes in a literary world that has been inundated with AI. In a 2026 survey Medium conducted with The Harris Poll, 71 percent of writers said they'd used AI in their writing process in the previous three months, yet 69 percent said they distrusted AI-assisted writing from others. TK brings AI closer into the writing process than ever before. Having an assistant a few keystrokes away could make it easier to churn out more AI slop...or the app could do what Stubblebine hopes and get writers sharing rough drafts and working through ideas together. As someone who reads a lot of AI slop these days, I'll be hoping for the latter.

#298. Why EV fast charging slows down after 80 percent

When the Tesla Model S launched in 2012, plugging it into a regular household outlet meant waiting more than 50 hours for a full charge, and all you got for that was up to 300 miles. The latest Model S is a different story: by the company's own figures, the car can add up to 200 miles in 15 minutes at a Supercharger. That's a very different car to live with.

But even now, charging doesn't stay fast. Look at how EV ads and spec sheets describe charging speed: "10 to 80 percent," "0 to 80 percent," almost never "to full." The first 80 percent is the fast part of the charge. Past it, the speed drops off, and the last stretch takes far longer than you'd expect. So what happens to the battery after 80 percent? It has nothing to do with bad EV charging habits, and everything to do with how the cell itself works.

There are three ways to charge an EV: via a regular wall socket, a home or public charging station or a fast-charge station. All three do the same basic thing. They push electrons toward the battery, and inside its cells, lithium ions travel from one side (the cathode) and settle into the other (the anode). Then, as the car drives, those ions travel back into the cathode and supply the electric current. So "charging the battery" just means pushing the ions back into the anode.

The difference is how much power each one can push. A wall socket gives you a trickle of electricity, and a home or public charging station gives you several times more. A fast-charge station typically delivers 50kW to 350kW, and the newest ones go higher. So those ions get pushed much faster.

There's a second difference that makes a fast-charge station much more efficient. A wall socket or a charging post only supplies alternating current, so the car's own onboard charger has to turn it into direct current for the battery, and that little converter caps the speed. A fast-charge station does the conversion itself, in a big cabinet that doesn't have to fit inside a car, and sends direct current straight to the battery. The car just tells it how much it can take.

Chargers push a constant current until the cell hits its voltage limit, then hold the voltage and let the current fall away. To see why that happens around 80 percent, picture the ions as passengers boarding an empty bus. They walk in one by one, find a seat and sit down. A faster charger just means more people come through the door each second. It doesn't add a single seat. Early on that works fine, because nearly every seat is free and everyone sits within a step or two of the door. Later the bus is mostly full. Each new passenger has to squeeze down the aisle, look for the last empty seat and wait for people ahead of them to shuffle along. Push more people through the door at that point and you just get a jam at the entrance.

A battery does the same thing. The anode is mostly graphite, and the lithium ions have to work their way into it, which is slow going. It takes even longer for the lithium to settle once the anode is nearly full. So past about 80 percent, the car asks the charger to cut the current, and the last stretch takes far longer than the first.

Ions aren't people on a bus, though, and that's where pushing harder goes wrong. If the charger forces current into an anode that's already crowded, the lithium ions pile up at the surface faster than they can work their way in. With nowhere to go, they settle on the graphite as a thin layer of metallic lithium. That's called lithium plating. It's partially irreversible, so the battery permanently loses lithium it needs, and the metal can grow into spikes that eventually cause a short circuit. So the car slows the charge down on purpose, long before anything bad happens.

BYD recently announced a battery that it says can charge up to 97 percent in nine minutes. Its answer to the problem was to make the bus easier to get through, instead of shoving the passengers harder. The second-generation Blade Battery, unveiled in March 2026, rebuilds the graphite so its particles stand upright, which gives the ions a short, straight path instead of a winding one. BYD also changed the liquid the ions travel through so they move more easily, and the other side of the battery lets them leave faster. The result, according to BYD, is lower internal resistance and less heat, so the battery can take far more current. BYD says that, paired with its 1,500kW Flash Charger, the battery goes from 10 to 70 percent in five minutes and 10 to 97 percent in nine. The slowdown is still there. It just starts much later and ends much sooner. Carmakers do slow the charge on purpose, but they're only doing it because of the physics inside the cell.

#297. Claude Code is accidentally exposing secrets all over GitHub

“Do not ask again” prompt leads to passwords, API keys, and more being shared.

Claude Code is leaking sensitive information on public GitHub instances thanks to a partial oversight in the way its settings files are handled. That’s the finding of AI security researchers at Apiiro, who discovered thousands of secrets, including database passwords, API tokens, and root logins, amid Claude Code settings files embedded in GitHub repositories.

The leak stems from the way Claude Code handles user approval for automated shell commands. When a user chooses the “Yes, and don’t ask again” option to approve running a command, that choice gets stored as part of a rule in the local “.claude/settings.local.json” file on the user’s computer. That rule is sometimes coded as a pattern, but the command itself is often written to that settings file as is, including any sensitive information that may be embedded within it.

This isn’t really a significant security issue if the settings file stays on the user’s local machine (assuming that machine doesn’t become compromised itself, of course). And Anthropic notes in its Claude Code documentation that this particular settings file gets added to the developer’s global .gitignore list, which usually prevents it from being shared publicly.

But that .gitignore setting isn’t reflected at the repo level, meaning the sensitive file can be uploaded accidentally if a repo is cloned to another machine or a folder becomes a repo later, for instance. In many cases, Claude Code itself will commit changes to the settings file without the user’s input, Apiiro writes.

Apiiro’s researchers found over 100,000 cases where Claude Code’s local settings file had been uploaded to a public GitHub repository. Of these, only about 1,100 from 63 different organizations contained secrets such as public API tokens, database passwords, or private keys. Even after these files are discovered and removed from a repository, the secrets can persist in forks of the original project if a GitHub user isn’t careful.

Although this security issue is getting more attention now, it was flagged in a Claude Code bug report as far back as August 2025. Other bug reports also noted that the sensitive settings file was sometimes not added to the user’s .gitignore list or that Claude Code could add the sensitive commands to another file that was not protected from git sharing. These bug reports were all closed without action after 60 days of inactivity.

In a comment thread on Hacker News over the weekend, Claude Code’s Boris Cherny said the team is “looking into what we can do to prevent this.” That said, he noted that these settings files should pretty much never be checked in to a user’s git source control and that cases like these seemed to come up only when “the files were manually created and checked in.”

In the meantime, Cherny recommended using Claude Code’s auto mode, which uses classifiers to automatically route “routine permission prompts” without requiring what Cherny calls “manually maintaining finicky allow/deny lists.” But when another user noted that sensitive commands can still be saved to settings files in auto mode, Cherny said that was a “good catch” and added that the team is “thinking through what we can do to improve this.”

Cherny also recommends Claude Code users make use of secret scanners—which can trawl files for sensitive information and scrub it out—and said that the team was considering building this kind of secret scanning into Claude Code itself in the future. That said, the Apiiro researchers found that 215 of the roughly 1,100 public secrets it found in Claude Code settings files were not flagged by two popular secret scanners, gitleaks and TruffleHog.

This is far from the only case of an AI agent unintentionally leaking sensitive information. Last month, Meta suspended the sharing feature on its Muse agent after security researchers found the URLs for those links could be easily guessed.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#296. Kwame Brathwaite, photographer of 'Black is Beautiful' movement, dies at 85

Editor’s Note:  This article was originally published by The Art Newspaper, an editorial partner of CNN Style.

Kwame Brathwaite, the pioneering activist and photographer whose work helped define the aesthetics of the “Black is Beautiful” movement of the 1960s and beyond, died on April 1, aged 85.

His son, Kwame Brathwaite, Jr, announced his father’s death in an Instagram post that read in part, “I am deeply saddened to share that my Baba, the patriarch of our family, our rock and my hero has transitioned.”

Brathwaite’s work has been the subject of resurgent interest from curators, historians and collectors in recent years, and his first major institutional retrospective, which was organized by the Aperture Foundation, made its debut in 2019 at the Skirball Cultural Center in Los Angeles before touring the country.

Brathwaite was born in 1938 to Barbadian immigrants, in what he referred to as “the People’s Republic of Brooklyn” in New York, though his family moved from there to Harlem and then to the South Bronx when Brathwaite was 5 years old. He attended the School of Industrial Art (now the High School of Art and Design) and, according to profiles of Brathwaite in T Magazine and Vice, was drawn to photography by two moments. The first was in August of 1955, when a 17-year-old Brathwaite encountered David Jackson’s haunting photograph of a brutalized Emmett Till in his open casket. The second was in 1956, when — after he and his brother Elombe co-founded the African Jazz Arts Society and Studios (AJASS) — Brathwaite saw a young man taking photos in a dark jazz club without the use of a flash, and his mind became alight with possibility.

Using a Hasselblad medium-format camera, Brathwaite attempted to do the same, learning to work with limited light in a manner that enhanced the visual narrative of his imagery. He would soon also develop a darkroom technique that enriched and deepened how Black skin would appear in his photography, honing the practice in a small darkroom in his Harlem apartment. Brathwaite went on to photograph jazz legends performing throughout the 1950s and ’60s, including Miles Davis, John Coltrane, Thelonious Monk and others.

“You want to get the feeling, the mood that you’re experiencing when they’re playing,” Brathwaite told Aperture Magazine in 2017. “That’s the thing. You want to capture that.”

A 106-year-old from the Philippines is Vogue's oldest ever cover model

By the early 1960s, alongside the rest of AJASS, Brathwaite began using his photography and organizing prowess to consciously push back against whitewashed, Eurocentric beauty standards. The group came up with the concept of the Grandassa Models, young Black women whom Brathwaite would photograph, celebrating and accentuating their features. In 1962, AJASS organized “Naturally ’62”, a fashion show held in a Harlem club called the Purple Manor and featuring the models. The show would go on to be held regularly until 1992. In 1966, Brathwaite married his wife Sikolo, a Grandassa Model whom he had met on the street the year prior when he asked if he could take her portrait. The two remained married for the rest of Brathwaite’s life.

By the 1970s, Brathwaite’s focus on jazz shifted to other forms of popular Black music. In 1974, he traveled to Africa with the Jackson Five to document their tour, also photographing the historic “Rumble in the Jungle” boxing match between Muhammad Ali and George Foreman in what’s now the Democratic Republic of Congo that same year. Commissions in this era also saw Brathwaite photographing Nina Simone, Stevie Wonder, Sly and the Family Stone, Bob Marley and other music legends.

Creating for community: The Black designers placing identity and heritage at the center of their brands

Throughout the ensuing decades, Brathwaite continued to explore and develop his mode of photography, all through the lens of the “Black is Beautiful” ethos. In 2016, Brathwaite joined the roster of Philip Martin Gallery in Los Angeles, and he was continuing to photograph commissions as recently as 2018, when he shot artist and stylist Joanne Petit-Frère for The New Yorker.

T Magazine’s 2021 profile, published on the occasion of Brathwaite’s retrospective traveling to the Blanton Museum of Art in Austin, Texas, noted that the photographer’s health was failing such that he was unable to be interviewed for the article. A separate exhibition, “Kwame Brathwaite: Things Well Worth Waiting For,” is currently on view at the Art Institute of Chicago, where it will remain until July 24.

Top image: Kwame Brathwaite, “Untitled (Sikolo Brathwaite, Orange Portrait),” 1968

The receipt-scanning feature is rolling out now.

No more trying to figure out who's paying for the bill and who's going to Venmo who later. #292. Pentagon mulls placing fleet of military satellites around the Moon

“Please jump on my cislunar bandwagon because I need some more friends here.”

The US military has published a Request for Information on a federal procurement website signaling interest in deploying a “proliferated cislunar constellation” consisting of an unspecified number of satellites performing an undisclosed mission in the vicinity of the Moon.

The announcement follows statements in recent months from the Space Force’s acquisitions chief for advanced programs, Maj. Gen. Stephen Purdy, that the military was getting serious about extending its reach to the Moon. The Space Force’s most distant satellites today are in geostationary orbit, some 22,000 miles (36,000 kilometers) over the equator, collecting routine surveillance and missile warning data.

Most recently, Purdy spoke of a shift in Pentagon leadership’s thinking about the Moon following an executive order signed by President Donald Trump last December. The order set out the Trump administration’s space policy. One section of the order deals with the US government’s ability to detect, characterize, and counter threats to US space interests from low-Earth orbit to cislunar space, the term of art for the region of space around the Moon.

“I’ve got the Secretary of War and the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs full on stating in public: ‘This is a warfighting domain,’” Purdy said at Space Systems Command’s Industry Days event in October, according to a report in Air & Space Forces Magazine. “And so, I would ask you all, please jump on my cislunar bandwagon because I need some more friends here, along with our deep, passionate crew that have been working on this for years.”

Officials have emphasized the military’s role at the Moon is to protect US interests there as the United States and China ramp up their lunar programs, with a series of robotic missions culminating in crewed lunar landings in the next few years. Some US officials foresee a competition for real estate and resources at the Moon, where there are a finite number of safe landing zones that can access the Moon’s water ice at the south pole.

“We have a role to play, but we’re not the lead,” said Gen. Chance Saltzman, who was the top general in the Space Force until his retirement in August. “I just don’t want to get caught flat-footed when we start to have to protect US interests out there, so we’re along for the ride.”

Now, the Pentagon has taken a preliminary step toward a “Cislunar Space System,” a constellation of military satellites the military says it is considering deploying around the Moon. The new RFI was released by the Department of the Air Force and dated November 2. It asks companies to inform the military on their ability to provide “rapid” and “low-risk” solutions to support the government’s “technical and programmatic objectives.”

The document provided few details about what the Pentagon wants to do around the Moon with the Cislunar Space System, other than it will consist of up to five spacecraft, each with a government-provided payload, to meet military “mission needs” in late 2029.

The RFI hints at a vision for a much larger fleet of satellites in cislunar space. The first five satellites “may serve as seed vehicles that will inform the acquisition strategy for a potential future proliferated cislunar constellation,” the RFI said.

“The government intends to award up to five firm fixed price preliminary design study contracts upon government consideration of the market information collected with this RFI and all other sources of information relevant to the effort,” military officials wrote on the government procurement website.

Responses are due by November 24. It is unclear when the Pentagon might move forward with firm contracts, and the language in the RFI does not commit the military to doing so. The standard commercial satellite development cycle can run two or three years, so it’s a safe bet that military officials would want deals signed sometime in 2027 to meet their 2029 schedule goal.

The secretary of the Air Force announced in September that the US military has placed unspecified weapons in orbit. But don’t expect the Space Force to send weapons to the Moon any time soon.

While NASA focuses its lunar program on human landings, a Moon Base, resource exploration, and scientific research, military satellites at the Moon could perform similar roles as they do in Earth orbit. These functions could include tracking and characterization of other objects in cislunar space, navigation and communications, and surveillance.

The Defense Department has already had a presence at the Moon. A joint NASA-DOD spacecraft named Clementine discovered the first hints of water ice inside permanently shadowed craters at the lunar south pole.

A military-led mission named Oracle-Mobility is scheduled to launch in mid-2027 on a trajectory toward the Moon. The $75 million Oracle-M mission, formerly known as the Defense Deep Space Sentinel, is led by the Air Force Research Laboratory, which says the satellite will be “highly mobile and will transit throughout cislunar space and experiment with techniques to maintain custody of objects in this region.”

“This mission will allow AFRL to assess strengths and weaknesses of navigation techniques needed for future operational systems,” AFRL says on its website.

A second Oracle mission, Oracle-Prime, is supposed to be ready for launch by the end of 2027. Its mission will involve detecting and tracking other satellites and debris from a position near Earth-Moon Lagrange Point 1, a gravitationally stable location about three-quarters of the way to the Moon.

Each of the Oracle missions will only involve a single spacecraft, but the recent RFI from the Department of the Air Force suggests a move toward a more sustained military presence around the Moon.

#291. What the tiny blue dot on your Android phone is telling you

You're scrolling through your phone when you spot it: a small blue dot sitting quietly near your battery icon. It wasn't there a minute ago, and now it's gone again just as fast. If you've wondered if it's a glitch, don't worry — we all have. It's not a notification you missed or even a problem or a bug. The blue dot is actually one of Android's built-in security features designed to keep you informed about what's happening on your device in real time. More specifically, it tells you when an app is accessing your location data.

Rather than burying this information in a settings menu you'd have to dig through, Android makes it easy to see right alongside all your other notification icons. The blue dot matters more than it might seem because location data is some of the most sensitive information your phone collects. It can reveal where you live, work, buy your groceries and spend your free time. Apps access this data for legitimate reasons, like turn-by-turn directions for your driving companion or local weather. But if you see the dot flashing on and off when you haven't opened a navigation app, you may want to do a bit of sleuthing.

The blue dot is Android's visual indicator that an app is actively tracking your device's location. It typically pops up in the top-right corner of your screen beside other status icons like Wi-Fi, battery and signal bars. When it shows up, an app on your phone is pulling location data through GPS, Wi-Fi signals or nearby cellular towers to figure out where you are.

Basically, the dot isn't meant to track every historical instance of location access, but rather to flag what's happening right now or what just happened moments ago. If you spot the dot while you're using a maps app, that's perfectly expected. If it pops up when you haven't touched an app or website that should need your location, it's time to dig more.

Swiping down from the top of your screen and tapping the indicator typically reveals which app triggered it, giving you an immediate answer without having to guess.

One small mention here — older One UI versions on Samsung phones only displayed a location pin icon in the status bar. Starting with One UI 9, however, the brand adopted the same blue pin the Pixel gets.

For apps that genuinely need location to function well, like weather services or navigation tools, setting permissions to "allow only while using the app" seems reasonable as you'll still get accurate directions or local forecasts. The app won't be able to track your movements once you close it, and that's reassuring.

#290. Study finds slightly higher risk of autism diagnosis in areas with more lithium in drinking water, but experts say more research is needed

A new study found a moderately higher risk of autism spectrum disorder in children born to pregnant people exposed to tap water with higher levels of lithium, but experts caution that this association does not show a direct link between the two.

About 1 in 36 children in the US is diagnosed with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) each year, according to data from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Scientists still don’t know the exact cause of autism, a developmental disorder. Genetics may be a factor, but some have been looking at potential environmental causes, too.

Autism diagnosis has become more common, but pandemic disrupted early detection, CDC says

Cases may be on the rise, but that is also unclear. One study published this year on cases in the New York-New Jersey area found that autism diagnosis rates tripled among certain age groups between 2000 and 2016. A 2021 report found similar increases in cases, but the CDC says the increased number of cases is most likely linked to more doctors screening for the condition.

Lithium is an alkali metal that can be found naturally in some food and ground water. It’s used in batteries, grease and air conditioners, as well as in the treatment of bipolar disorder and some blood disorders. Its levels in US drinking water are not regulated, according to the US Geological Survey.

A new study, published Monday in the journal JAMA Pediatrics, found a small association between lithium and autism diagnosis in Denmark, where the researchers say the level of lithium in drinking water is similar to that in American water systems.

The researchers checked a database of people with psychiatric disorders for children born between 2000 and 2013 to find information on 8,842 cases of ASD and 43,864 participants who did not have ASD. They then measured the concentration of lithium in 151 public waterworks that served more than half of the Danish population and mapped out where pregnant people lived in relation.

For many neurodiverse people, 'The Sims' has been a lifelong comfort

As lithium levels in water increased, there was a modest increased risk of an ASD diagnosis. Specifically, compared with people at the lowest exposure level, those who had the second and third highest exposure during pregnancy had a 24% to 26% higher risk of ASD diagnosed in children. The group with the highest exposure had a 46% higher risk than those at the lowest level of exposure.

The researchers could not tell how much water the pregnant people drank, but they picked Denmark in part because residents there consume some of the lowest amounts of bottled water in Europe.

Experts say it’s important to note that the research can’t show that lithium exposure leads directly to an autism diagnosis.

Further study is required, said study co-author Dr. Beate Ritz, a professor of neurology in the David Geffen School of Medicine at UCLA, and a professor of epidemiology and environmental health at the UCLA Fielding School of Public Health.

“Any drinking water contaminants that may affect the developing human brain deserve intense scrutiny,” Ritz said in a news release. She added that the research would need to be replicated in other countries to look for a similar connection.

The implications of the findings are complex as far as public health policy is concerned, according to an editorial published alongside the study. Lithium levels in water, at concentrations that the study associated with a potential ASD risk, have also been linked with health benefits such as lower rates of hospitalization for psychiatric disorders and suicide.

“If all these of associations are valid, the wisdom of Solomon will be required to develop guidelines for lithium in drinking water that are maximally protective of the entire population,” wrote Dr. David C. Bellinger, a professor of neurology and psychology at Harvard Medical School. “Until the basic biology of ASD is better understood, it will be difficult to distinguish causal from spurious associations.”

'Heartbreaking' delays in autism diagnosis and treatment got even worse during pandemic

Dr. Max Wiznitzer, director of the Rainbow Autism Center at University Hospitals Rainbow Babies and Children’s Hospital in Cleveland, points to other research on the effects of lithium on pregnant people who take it for mental health disorders. Those studies – which look at people exposed to much higher levels than are found in drinking water – do not show a connection with autism spectrum disorder.

“It’s an interesting association, but causation is definitely not proven,” said Wiznitzer, who was not involved in the new research. “We have to see if there’s a viable and biologically plausible mechanism by which a small amount of lithium in the water supply can somehow do this, yet pharmacologic dosing of lithium in women with bipolar disorder has not been reported to be causing increased risk of ASD.”

Other studies have also suggested connections between ASD and environmental exposures to things like pesticides, air pollution and phthalates. But none of them points to any of these factors as a direct cause of the disorder.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

A link between environmental exposure and ASD is hard to prove, Wiznitzer said. With research showing that increased exposure to air pollution raises the risk of giving birth to a child with ASD, for example, he often wonders whether pollution is the determining factor or if it’s just the populations who live in more polluted areas.

“There’s a lot of speculation about about environmental factors, but how many of them are truly causally associated?” Wiznitzer said. “We are bombarded with a variety of environmental stressors in our everyday lives. We have to figure out how to basically safely navigate them, and this is probably not one that’s high on our list.”

#288. The reason your laptop can get hot after extended use

Laptops can sometimes run hot, but there are ways to keep them cool.

It's not uncommon to notice your laptop suddenly getting hot, especially after long periods of use. The reason? There are many components inside — such as the processor and battery — packed into a relatively thin chassis, and all that heat has to go somewhere. This heat is often completely normal and caused by things like demanding tasks or background tabs. In these cases, the laptop usually returns to its normal state after a while.

However, if the temperatures get considerably hot and cause your laptop to freeze and restart, there may be something wrong. Luckily, there are ways to figure out the main causes and prevent your laptop from getting too hot.

Every component inside your laptop generates heat, and when you're running more demanding workflows, that heat tends to build up quickly. Playing 3D games or editing videos are just a couple examples of workflows that place greater demands on the hardware. But sometimes, even background processes and startup programs can quietly keep the CPU and memory busy, too. Not only that, but ordinary tasks like installing Windows or macOS updates also increase the load on the hardware, leading to high temperatures and causing the fans to spin faster.

The thing is, these are all completely normal situations. When you close these resource-intensive programs, the laptop tends to cool down within a few minutes. Internal sensors monitor the temperature of the laptop's components to determine when to make the fans spin faster or when to turn them off. Depending on the temperatures, these sensors can also trigger other built-in safeguards to force the laptop to cool down, such as temporarily reducing the speed of the CPU and GPU — a process known as throttling.

Airflow is important for keeping your laptop running at normal temperatures. Most laptop makers don't recommend using them in bed or on a pillow, since the fabric can block the air vents. High external temperatures can also have a major impact on your laptop's heat. Apple, for example, recommends keeping your MacBook between 50 and 95 degrees Fahrenheit, while Microsoft says the Surface's fans will run at a higher speed in environments above 77 degrees Fahrenheit.

Start by checking to see if anything is blocking your laptop's air vents — using it on a flat surface is always the best choice when it comes to preventing overheating. If you're constantly working with resource-intensive software, it might be worth looking into cooling pads, which are trays with fans that sit under your laptop to keep it cooler. If the air vents look dirty, gently clean them with a soft cloth, a brush or compressed air, but never poke anything directly into the vents.

Another way to cool down your laptop is to check for any background processes that are using a lot of CPU power. In Windows, press Ctrl + Shift + Esc to open Task Manager and sort the list by CPU usage to find what's slowing down the processor. There's also a Startup tab that lets you prevent programs from opening automatically when you turn on your laptop. For MacBook users, use Spotlight to open the Activity Monitor app and click the CPU tab. If there are any programs using a lot of CPU resources, try closing them to see if your laptop cools down. Also, there are third-party apps that help you monitor the temperature of your computer's CPU and other components.

If none of this helps, you may need technical support to see if there's something wrong with your laptop. Something might be blocking the fans from working, or the CPU thermal paste might have dried out completely. Another thing to keep in mind is that older laptops tend to overheat more quickly, since they can no longer keep up with the latest software. In that case, your best options are to downgrade to older software or start thinking about getting a new laptop.

#286. How does Google Wallet's notification feed work and can you disable it?

Here's how to check if you've received one of Google Wallet's latest feature rollouts.

If you've looked over the pros and cons of digital wallets and settled on Google's Wallet app, you'll be happy to know an update has dropped for the platform. This update, which began to roll out in October 2026, launched both increased passport recognition and an in-application notification inbox. This update has been added in an effort to have Google Wallet outdo Apple Wallet.

If you've received this new update, the notification feed is easy to find. However, if the thought of more notifications on your phone has you shivering, there are ways to keep this inbox quiet.

If you have Google Wallet's new notification feed feature, there should be a bell icon next to your profile icon in the top-right of the app. If you click on this bell icon, you'll be brought to the notification feed. This page will be totally blank with a bell in the middle if you have no notifications. Otherwise, you'll find your notifications listed here. Remember, the feed is still being rolled out, so you might not be able to find this page at all.

Any notifications you receive about updates to your saved cards and passes will be stored here. This includes changes to traveling information (such as a gate change on a boarding pass), loyalty and gift cards (like point balance updates), incoming offers and more. This notification feed page will simply aggregate these notifications in one place, allowing you to keep track of them from within the app.

One of the reasons you might be happy to see this new notification feed could be rooted in a common problem with Google Wallet, that being the sheer quantity of notifications the app sends. The feed will allow you to find them all in one organized place.

However, depending on your settings, Google Wallet might be taking advantage of Nearby Notifications. Some cards saved to Google Wallet might be sending you offer updates depending on your closeness to a certain location. If you want to avoid notifications from your wallet, this notification feed isn't the thing you want to target. Rather, you want to disable notifications entirely or remove Google's ability to use location data to ping you. You only need to tap on your profile picture, head to Wallet settings > Manage Wallet Notifications and choose the settings you want to tweak. You can also head to Settings > Apps > Google Wallet and either choose Notifications to turn all notifications off or Permissions to deny Google Wallet from using your location.

#285. Gmail users should know about this little-noticed feature

You can unsend messages on Gmail, but you only have seconds to act.

Whenever you hit Send on Gmail, a small box pops up in the bottom left corner of your screen that will let you undo the action before it's too late. It says Message sent along with the options Undo and View message. If you've suddenly spotted a typo or remembered a piece of information that you forgot to include in your original message, the "unsend" feature could be your best of friend. However, it only shows up for a very short of amount of time, and you have to hit Undo very, very quickly.

To be clear, the feature doesn't literally "unsend" an email that has already been sent. Once it's out there, you can't claw it back from your recipient's inbox, even if it hasn't been opened yet. What it does is delay the service from dispatching your email to give you the chance to stop it from going through. That is why you're only given a short amount of time to decide. Since it only delays your email from being sent, your intended recipient doesn't get notified about it and will not know that you've unsent an email. Take note that hitting View message before the waiting time ends also means you're ending your chance early to unsend your email.

The good news is that you can lengthen the amount of time you have to hit Undo, so you can give your messages a quick final read before Gmail pushes them through. You'll have to manage your expectations, though: Even if you choose the longest amount of time available, it's still pretty short. And no, you still can't take back emails that have already landed in your recipient's inbox.

To choose and extend the amount of time you have to unsend a message, go to Gmail on desktop and click or tap on the Gear icon at the top right corner of the screen. In the Quick settings panel, choose See all settings. In the General settings page, scroll down until you see the Undo Send section, where you'll find the option to change your cancellation period.

It will be set to the feature's default of five seconds, but you can change it to 10, 20 or 30 seconds. That half-a-minute period is the longest you can delay your email's dispatch, but it could be long enough to prevent disaster when sending out especially important messages. Don't forget to hit Save changes at the bottom of the General settings page for the new cancellation period to take effect.
