#253. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

#249. Musk says Tesla will refund some Cybertruck deposits, defends decision to end production

Tesla CEO Elon Musk is defending the Cybertruck and the company’s decision to stop producing the polarizing steel-clad truck, claiming that the order rate far exceeds its production.

Tesla will shut down production of the Cybertruck in about eight months Musk said in an X post on Monday. He also said the company will try to make as many of the vehicles as possible before then, though Tesla will “have to return deposits for a lot of people.”

Musk argued the Cybertruck was not unprofitable, and that Tesla was ending production because the “opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck is vastly outweighed by the value of incremental Cybercab production.”

In other words, Musk is arguing that Tesla can make more money by using the factory space and resources dedicated to the Cybertruck for building Cybercab robotaxis. Musk said last week that Tesla plans to use the factory space to build more two-seater Cybercabs.

The first Cybertrucks were delivered to customers in late 2023, when it seemed like it could be a breakthrough hit for the company. Reservations for the vehicle had ballooned to 1 million, according to Musk, who also predicted that the company would sell around 250,000 of the trucks annually.

But those reservations never translated into actual sales. Tesla doesn’t break out sales of the Cybertrucks, but industry experts estimate a total of about 71,000 Cybertrucks have been sold since the first deliveries began in November 2023.

Many of those sales came from inside Musk’s ecosystem of other businesses. For instance, SpaceX (now known as SpaceXSI), which he also leads and is the largest shareholder of, purchased $131 million worth of Cybertrucks from Tesla in 2025, according to the company’s S-1 registration statement ahead of its IPO. Another Musk company, xAI, also bought $430 million worth of Tesla Megapacks for its data centers, before it merged with SpaceX.

But sales of the Cybertruck, which lagged for months, could see a final bump now that it’s going out of production. Still, even a scarcity boost is unlikely to push the truck’s demand to the lofty projections from several years ago.

It has a built-in AI assistant to do things like “start a new song, set up a vocal track from a single prompt, or refine an active project.”

These Intelligence Usage Packs, as Apple calls them, will allow users to continue accessing certain Apple Intelligence features that have usage limits because they rely on cloud-based AI models from OpenAI or Google Gemini. Currently, only Logic Pro has the option to buy more usage, but it’s expected to be added to other Apple Creator Studio apps this month, including Pixelmator Pro, Final Cut Pro, Motion, plus Pages, Keynote, and Numbers that are getting access to Apple’s AI chatbot.

#247. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#245. The global pledge to use AI for climate good is a cruel joke

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A pledge to make AI less of an environmental disaster is floating around United Nations climate negotiations this month, but it’s just lip service.

A pledge to make AI less of an environmental disaster is floating around United Nations climate negotiations this month, but it’s just lip service.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

At this point, reading through the latest splashy pledge to use AI for the good of the environment feels like downing a nonalcoholic beer. Admittedly, I’m suspicious from the onset. How can this be good? But I love beer and our planet enough to give it a whirl — and hey, why not try a little harm reduction?

Negotiators from more than 190 countries around the world are in Antalya, Turkey, this month for the annual United Nations climate summit, called COP31 (the 31st “Conference of the Parties” that ratified the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change). Turkey previewed its Antalya Artificial Intelligence Pledge ahead of the convening, inviting delegates from other countries to endorse the pledge before its official launch on November 16th.

Like that nonalcoholic beer, it’s deeply unsatisfying

The pledge actually includes some common sense measures that could curb planet-heating pollution from energy-hungry AI data centers. Unfortunately, like that nonalcoholic beer, it’s deeply unsatisfying.

At first glance, many of the ingredients you’d expect from a real commitment on AI’s environmental impact are here. It sets out to ensure that data centers are increasingly powered by clean energy, calling for internationally recognized standards for responsible AI development. Similarly, it pushes for reporting requirements so that there’s greater transparency into the impact data centers — which run primarily on fossil fuels — already have on the environment. It even acknowledges the need to manage the tons of e-waste created by the generative AI boom.

And yet, there is no there there because, as the text states clearly, “these objectives of the pledge are voluntary and non-prescriptive, create no new obligations, and are to be implemented in accordance with national circumstances.” In other words, these are voluntary commitments that countries can choose to follow or not follow based on their whims.

There’s also no way this pledge will garner the support of the most influential player in this game (at least for now). The US has way more data centers than any other country, but Donald Trump has pulled the nation out of the global climate accord and has no official delegates at the summit in Turkey.

The last point in the pledge is to ensure that advances in generative AI can be used to create more renewable energy and reduce greenhouse gas emissions, particularly in economically developing countries. To that end, research published in a Nature journal in August found that AI causes more carbon emissions by boosting fossil fuel production than it would help avoid through gains in renewable energy.

When it comes down to it, this pledge offers a placebo effect at best. In the face of data centers demanding tremendous amounts of electricity and spurring the development of more fossil fuel infrastructure, negotiators can’t be seen as doing nothing about the issue. And with global average temperatures still on the rise, we could all use an emotional support beverage.

#243. Automattic debuts a platform to deploy vibe-coding apps amid a changing internet

WordPress has been a crucial technology and content management system for websites like TechCrunch. Now, WordPress.com owner Automattic is responding to the changing state of the web by debuting a new service called Spacefast, which will allow users to deploy vibe-coded websites and apps.

Spacefast is designed to provide infrastructure for the vibe-coded apps, including databases, authentication, file uploads, emails, and analytics, to make them functional websites, apps, or services. The product competes with the likes of Lovable and Emergent, which offer both interface and rails for vibe-coded apps, but in a more open way.

Users can connect Spacefast with other agents and chatbots like ChatGPT, Claude, Hermes, OpenClaw, Grok Bot, Muse, Pi, Cursor, OpenCode, and Amp. Alternatively, they can connect a Git repository to Spacefast to get started.

The product is headed by Batuhan İçöz, who worked on it during one of the company’s hackathon sprints earlier in the year. At that time, he worked on the company’s cross-platform messaging app Beeper and said that he often got Vercel links from his non-technical friends while exploring vibe coding.

“I thought there had to be a better way for everyone to set up and deploy their projects. Automattic already has good infrastructure, but we have been traditionally associated with just WordPress. I thought, why do I have to use Cloudflare when we have a CDN (content delivery network) just as good? So I started working on what was called Stattic and now Spacefast,” he told TechCrunch over a call.

İçöz eventually disseminated the solution to his friends and added more features to make the product more dynamic. Automattic soft-launched the product in September to get feedback before today’s launch. The company has seen varied use cases of their product, with people and organizations using it for internal mockups, slideshows, and offer-related websites. However, that’s not the only competition Spacefast has. Productivity and design software like Figma, Canva, and Notion have launched products that sit at the intersection of vibe coding, mockups, and presentations.

İçöz thinks that, compared to something like Lovable, Spacefast has the advantage of giving users a choice of their agent and using the solution their way. The counter-argument might be that a certain kind of user wouldn’t want to choose and just have everything figured out for them.

Spacefast said that it also has the advantage of being within Automattic to launch solutions like web analytics, WordPress integration, and e-commerce features (through WooCommerce) easily to compete better with the whole lot.

“For enterprises, the problem right now is IT sprawl, as their marketing teams are churning out landing pages on services like Lovable, and they want to consolidate and work with a company that understands the enterprise needs. With our existing products, we have experience serving enterprise customers,” İçöz noted.

A challenge for newly minted products in the AI world is to retain people post-launch. Users might get excited to try out a new product, and then move on to the new shiny thing within days or weeks. İçöz is cognizant of the fact and said that he expects people to churn, as they might not be interested in making apps at all.

“I think what we need to do as a product is make sure that when you build something with SpaceFast, it is so easy, and the results are so much better than without Spacefast,” İçöz emphasized.

Many websites and publishers are struggling with dwindling traffic due to AI chatbots and summaries, right now, to the point that Google itself saw its ad revenue growth drop. İçöz acknowledged that people are making websites with ChatGPT or Claude now as compared to deploying something on WordPress, and he said some sites won’t need a content management system at all as the web expands.

Spacefast comes amid turbulent months at Automattic. In an OpenAI/Sam Altman-style saga, the company’s board forced CEO Matt Mullenweg out, but he was back in control within days. The rebel board was eventually replaced, and the interim CFO left within weeks of appointment. In October, TechCrunch reported that the company had begun layoffs across several properties and more cuts were expected.

#239. Meet Drops, the new music app bringing back the fun of sharing songs with friends

There’s a new music discovery app borrowing the endless vertical-scrolling format of TikTok. But instead of watching videos, users swipe through short clips of songs, hoping to stumble upon their next favorite artist.

Launched Monday by Harman, a Samsung subsidiary, Drops aims to make discovering new music feel social again.

Available on iOS and Android in the U.S., the app lets users scroll through 10- to 30-second snippets of songs, accompanied by lyrics, album artwork, artist photos, and captions. The idea is to give listeners just enough of a track to grab their attention, whether it’s a particularly good lyric, a catchy hook, or a beat that makes them want to hear more.

The company says Drops is meant to feel like a return to how people used to discover music in the first place. Before every platform’s algorithm recommendations, people often heard about new songs through friends, music blogs, or online communities. Drops is trying to recreate that experience.

“You send a friend a song and say, ‘You have to hear this.’ Then you end up texting, ‘Skip to 1:47, that’s the best part.’ The part that hits usually isn’t the first few seconds, and there wasn’t a good way to share just that moment,” Clare Hunter, general manager of Drops, told us.

Hunter explained that the idea for Drops began taking shape after Harman acquired music management software company Roon in 2023.

“I used to spend hours making mixtapes for my friends, picking the songs, the order, and decorating the cover. Drops is the version of that I always wanted,” Hunter added.

The app is easy to navigate, with tabs including “For You,” “New,” “Showcase,” and “Following,” along with relevant and trending hashtags.

Users can like, comment, repost, share, and save tracks to collections called Droplists. They can also send songs directly to other users, follow other profiles, and exchange direct messages.

To listen to the entire track, Drops offers integrations with major streaming services, including Apple Music, Qobuz, and TIDAL. Users can jump directly into the full version of the song through their connected streaming platform.

Notably, the company says it doesn’t use generative AI for audio or visuals. Songs appear as they were originally released, using official album artwork and licensed artist photography. At a time when AI-generated music and slop are becoming harder to avoid, this will likely appeal to many users.

Another part worth noting is that Drops pays rights holders and reports plays back to its partners. Those partners include Universal Music Group, Beggars Group, Empire, Symphonic Distribution, and UnitedMasters.

Plays also count toward streams on those services, meaning Drops isn’t trying to replace existing music subscriptions.

Drops is currently invite-only, with U.S. users able to join the waitlist.

#237. Everything we know so far about the Nvidia spy scandal targeting CEO Jensen Huang

The leather-jacketed CEO of Nvidia, Jensen Huang, has acknowledged an affair with Megaspeed exec Alice Huang, spurring an investigation by his company and intense discussions worldwide. Recent reporting noted that since last year, US intelligence reports had identified Alice Huang (no relation) as not only the exec for an AI compute company renting access to Nvidia GPUs, but also a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security.

In recent years, Nvidia has become a key part of the Trump administration’s AI push, a central figure in negotiations over potential Chinese access to cutting-edge AI hardware, and a company that has used its now $4 trillion-plus market cap to finance other AI companies, including the “neoclouds” like Coreweave who have been some of its biggest customers. So far, Nvidia and Jensen Huang have said that they have “no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised,” but that hasn’t settled things as the investigation continues.

It’s difficult to overstate Nvidia’s power in today’s AI landscape. The company’s chips are the bedrock upon which advanced AI technology in the US is being built, and demand has consistently outpaced supply for years. Nvidia has also been an influential investor in a wide range of up-and-coming AI companies in recent years, with about $99 billion worth of equity investments in frontier AI labs, neoclouds, infrastructure companies, and more. Its chief executive has even become close with President Donald Trump.

This is why, as both cofounder and longtime CEO of Nvidia, Jensen Huang would be a valuable target for a foreign adversary with sights on the American AI industry.

That was before she became “an executive” at Megaspeed, which bought $4.8 billion of hard-to-get Nvidia chips, starting around the time she was being photographed with Jensen in a Taipei bar in 2024. “To many in the industry, the well-funded push by Megaspeed into A.I. appeared to come out of nowhere,” The New York Times wrote, tactfully. #236. Waymo robotaxis kept illegally passing school buses after software fix, feds say

Nearly a year ago, Waymo updated its self-driving software and issued a recall after numerous reports that its robotaxis were illegally passing school buses that had stopped and deployed flashing red lights. Now, federal safety regulators have opened a new investigation into why that behavior has continued.

The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) said in a November 6 filing that a majority of these reported incidents occurred after the company deployed a software update intended to fix the problem.

Reuters was the first to report the filing.

Waymo said last year that a software update was completed for all affected vehicles on November 17, 2025. However, NHTSA said the majority of the incidents occurred after the software update. NHTSA’s Office of Defects Investigation (ODI) has opened a “recall query” to “assess the adequacy of the remedy.”

The ODI opened its initial investigation into Waymo in October 2025 after seeing footage of one of its autonomous vehicles maneuvering around a stopped school bus that had its stop sign extended, lights flashing, and was unloading children in Atlanta. In that incident, a Waymo robotaxi crossed perpendicularly in front of the school bus from its right side. The autonomous vehicle then turned left around the front of the bus before traveling down the street.

Numerous other incidents have been documented in the weeks and months since in Austin, where Waymo also operates a robotaxi service. Austin Independent School District officials reported at least five such incidents at the time, according to a letter filed with NHTSA.

Reached for comment, Waymo stressed the importance of safety to its mission. “We have made significant improvements to vehicle behavior around school buses and have received no citations since March 2026,” a spokesperson told TechCrunch via email. “The Waymo Driver has a clearly demonstrated positive impact on roadway safety. Once received, we’ll work to respond to NHTSA’s query and look forward to continuing our engagement on recall processes for AV software that continues to learn and improve.”

TechCrunch also reached out to Austin Independent School District officials for comment and will update the article if they respond.

This article was updated after publication with Waymo’s statement.

#233. Here’s what Trump said he knew about Jensen Huang

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

In a White House press conference, President Trump was vague about when learned of Jensen Huang’s affair.

In a White House press conference, President Trump was vague about when learned of Jensen Huang’s affair.

President Trump held a White House press briefing earlier today and responded to several questions about his knowledge of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy. Huang said this weekend that he had been “​​unfaithful” to his wife, and the company has opened an investigation into Huang.

Recent articles from The New York Times indicated that Trump had been aware of a relationship between Huang and Alice Huang, the suspected spy, before information about it became public. In October, The New York Times reported that Trump had been briefed “roughly a year ago” on intelligence that Huang and the alleged spy, Alice Huang, had developed a close relationship, but he didn’t do anything with the information. Yesterday, the NYT reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio helped draft a memo to Trump officials about the relationship before Trump visited China in May.

In the press conference, Trump wasn’t specific about when he learned of the affair. But Trump said Huang told him today that he had “no idea” the person was a spy.

Here are Trump’s responses to questions about Jensen Huang from his press briefing today. You can watch a replay of the press conference on MS Now’s YouTube channel.

When were you first informed that there was an issue with Jensen Huang and the affair? What was presented to you at the time?

I would say that when I heard about it, first of all, I was amazed because I view him as a very high-quality person. I’ve known him a long time. It’s the largest, by value, the largest or one of the largest companies in the world. He did that with a chip. When you think about it, you’ve got a chip that’s 10 years advanced, can’t be… the Blackwell can’t even be copied. He’s an amazing guy.

I just hope there’s no problem. If there is a problem, we’ll let you know about it. But I hope there’s no problem. I believe he’s suffering greatly. I read his statement. And I believe he’s suffering greatly. Moreso because of his family. His wife who he loves and his family. But he is definitely suffering.

Just to follow up on the, “When were you first told of these potential issues with Mr. Huang?” You didn’t answer that question.

Well I’ve been hearing about it, and I’ve been reading a little bit about it over a period of time. But, not too long ago.

We’ll look into it. I know the FBI’s looking into it in terms of something else. I think that he’ll be in good shape.

Are you going to bring up the case of Jensen Huang with President Xi when you visit with him next week?

No, I don’t think so. Maybe, I’m sure, President Xi will ask me about it. I just know Jensen very well. I just think he’s a very honorable man. He built a great company. American company. I think he’s a very honorable guy. I feel very confident that he’ll be in good shape.

Sticking with Jensen Huang, when you went to China earlier this year in May, did you know about these alleged ties to the spy then?

No, I didn’t hear about it then. But a lot of people have heard about it. And Jensen totally denies that. I asked him today, because I did speak to him today just to see if he’s feeling alright, how are you doing… and I asked him about that. He said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy. I asked him that question, did you have any idea that she was a spy? He said, I had no idea that she was a spy. And I really believe him.

I want to put a finer point on when exactly you were made aware of the intelligence around Jensen Huang.

Oh, I’d have to check. But this has been going on…

But there are reports that Secretary Rubio put an intelligence memo out last year. Did you read that memo?

I’d certainly check with the intelligence people. It could have been mentioned. Certainly it didn’t make much of an impact on me.

#232. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#231. Meta expands Instagram’s AI age-guessing tech globally

Meta announced on Monday that it’s expanding its AI-powered age-estimation technology on Instagram worldwide to identify more teens and place them under its Teen Account protections, as it faces numerous lawsuits accusing its platforms of harming teens.

The social media giant says it will use the AI technology to find people it suspects to be teens, even if they use an adult birthday.

Teen Accounts place users into a stricter account experience with additional safeguards, such as receiving direct messages only from people they follow or are already connected to, hiding harmful comments, and setting accounts to private by default.

Meta’s age-guessing tools use AI to analyze entire profiles for contextual clues, such as birthday celebrations or mentions of school grades. Meta also looks for these signals across different formats, such as posts, comments, bios, captions, and more. The company also looks at visual cues from photos and videos, including a person’s height or bone structure.

Meta said it’s continuing to roll out the technology on Facebook, with a similar global rollout planned for Threads later this year.

The global expansion comes as Meta reached settlements worth up to $18 billion with nearly every U.S. state and territory in August to resolve lawsuits alleging its platforms harm teens. As part of the settlements, Meta agreed to improve how it identifies teens and children under 13.

“We’ll continue to do our part to proactively ensure teens are in age-appropriate experiences on our apps,” Meta wrote in a blog post. “That’s why as part of our agreement with 52 bipartisan attorneys general across US states, territories, and the District of Columbia, we’re investing in even stronger technology to proactively catch accounts that may belong to children under 13. We’re also strengthening the technology we use to identify accounts that may belong to teens between the ages of 13 and 17 so we can ensure those accounts are placed in experiences designed for teens, even if they give us an adult birthday.”

Meta said parents in the U.S. will start seeing an in-feed video on Instagram explaining its approach to ensuring their teens are in age-appropriate protections.

#229. What could China get from Jensen Huang?

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nvidia CEO has been in a unique position to to influence AI export controls.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nvidia CEO has been in a unique position to to influence AI export controls.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It’s difficult to overstate Nvidia’s power in today’s AI landscape. The company’s chips are the bedrock upon which advanced AI technology in the US is being built, and demand has consistently outpaced supply for years. Nvidia has also been an influential investor in a wide range of up-and-coming AI companies in recent years, with about $99 billion worth of equity investments in frontier AI labs, neoclouds, infrastructure companies, and more. Its chief executive has even become close with President Donald Trump.

This is why, as both cofounder and longtime CEO of Nvidia, Jensen Huang would be a valuable target for a foreign adversary with sights on the American AI industry.

Huang is in hot water after news broke of his extramarital affair with an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang (no relation to Jensen) is an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based cloud provider and a top Nvidia customer. Questions are swirling around how the affair may have influenced Huang’s reported push to sell advanced chips to China — and potentially even influenced President Trump’s own decisions surrounding AI export controls.

Jensen Huang would be a valuable target for a foreign adversary with sights on the American AI industry

US officials believe the Megaspeed executive is a foreign intelligence asset that has funneled information straight to the Chinese government, per The New York Times — in particular, officials reportedly believe that Megaspeed is related to a convoluted web of Chinese companies working together to access chips in bulk and perhaps even illegally get some of those chips into China. Alibaba is reportedly one of Megaspeed’s top customers.

“It’s not entirely surprising that the CEO of arguably one of the most important, if not the most important, AI chip companies is the target of some sort of intelligence-gathering from countries like China,” Hanna Dohmen, a senior research analyst at CSET, said in an interview with The Verge, adding that an alleged spy would potentially be interested in “better understanding what the US government is thinking on export controls,” as well as gathering information on Huang’s conversations with the Trump administration, attempting to influence policy, and gathering information on trade secrets like “who’s buying Nvidia’s chips, who’s getting them first, what’s coming next in terms of AI chips.”

Megaspeed’s sister company, headquartered in Malaysia, spent $4.8 billion or more on Nvidia chips and technology between June 2024 and February 2026, the Times reported, citing trade records from trade platform ImportGenius. The transaction sizes were outside the norm for a small company, particularly when Nvidia chips are so difficult to get.

”Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job”

Nvidia spokespeople had said Huang’s relationship with the Megaspeed executive was strictly business. Their interactions were described as “not unusual or unexpected” in statements to the press. Nvidia’s line was, “Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job.”

But at some point, their relationship became personal. In June 2024, just before Megaspeed became an Nvidia customer, photos show the two with other AI executives at a bar in Taipei after a local trade show; the following year, they reportedly had a business dinner in Taipei with others as well. But in private, according to US officials cited by the Times, the two’s relationship raised alarm bells for the US government — they had reportedly been surveilling the Megaspeed executive for months, if not longer, and that’s how it came out that she was in close contact with Huang.

The revelation is particularly concerning because of Huang’s lobbying role. He’d been trying to convince the Trump administration and Congress to loosen export controls for sending advanced semiconductors to China, ostensibly so that Nvidia could sell more chips. We now know this was happening around the same time that Huang was spotted at the Taiwan dinner with Alice Huang in May 2025, according to Culper Research, which shorted Nvidia’s stock earlier this year. That dinner occurred shortly after Nvidia had shared the Trump administration’s new restrictions on its China-specific H20 chips in April.

Just a couple months after the Taiwan dinner, Huang reportedly met with Trump in the Oval Office in July 2025 to plead his case for loosening export restrictions on Nvidia’s advanced semiconductors. Days later, the company announced that the administration had blessed sales of its H20 chips to China. Huang had argued that spreading American chips to China would help establish US technology as the standard, rather than allowing for Chinese chips to grow, according to The New York Times. After setbacks earlier in the second Trump term in export policy to China, Huang began speaking with then-White House AI czar David Sacks in 2025, who became the key advocate for Nvidia’s view that selling American chips to China would be beneficial, the Times reported.

By October 2025, Huang was lobbying Trump to allow Nvidia to sell more advanced chips to China, ahead of Trump’s meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, per the Times; but after Trump considered the proposal, he ultimately decided not to move forward. (That same month, Huang hired an external PR firm known for handling sex scandals, Wired reported.)

Months later, in December 2025, Trump changed course slightly, allowing Nvidia to sell H20 chips to a greenlit list of “approved customers” in China, with the US government taking a 25 percent cut of those sales. The same rules would apply to other US chipmakers, he wrote on Truth Social at the time.

“It is in Nvidia’s interest and in Jensen Huang’s interest to lobby for looser export control restrictions”

CSET’s Dohmen said that during the past two years, the US has seen a light loosening of AI export control regulations under the Trump administration, driven partly by Huang’s own lobbying. But it’s important to note, she said, that “even without any kind of intelligence operations from the Chinese side, it is in Nvidia’s interest and in Jensen Huang’s interest to lobby for looser export control restrictions.” Any restrictions by definition stifle Nvidia’s business expansion and cut off potential sales to help it grow its already stunning market cap — which is currently about $4.25 trillion, second only to Apple.

Huang’s relationship with Trump is also particularly relevant here. The president lavished praise on Huang during a speech last year, saying that until recently, he hadn’t even heard of Nvidia, and then he wanted to break it up. But after meeting Huang, he said, he understood why it would be difficult to re-create the company he’d built. More recently, Huang put Trump on speakerphone when the president called him during an appearance onstage at the All-In Summit, publicly displaying his close access to the Oval Office.

This past spring, a classified memo — the writing of which was overseen by Secretary of State Marco Rubio, per the Times — circulated among Trump administration officials ahead of the president’s trip to China in May, warning of the Megaspeed executive’s alleged involvement in surveillance on behalf of China’s Ministry of State Security. Her reported goal was to learn about advanced AI chips in the US and try to influence them being made available to China on a large scale. Asked if he had known about the memo reviewed by Rubio at a press briefing on Monday, Trump said, “I’d certainly check with the intelligence people. It could have been mentioned. Certainly it didn’t make much of an impact on me.” Earlier in the briefing, he said he was unaware of the alleged spy connection ahead of his trip to China.

Huang was initially left off the list of executives joining Trump on the trip, but the president later asked him to join. Trump had been briefed about a year ago that US intelligence believed the Megaspeed executive to be a China intelligence operative, according to the Times, and that she had a close relationship with a US citizen who turned out to be Nvidia’s CEO. At the time, he reportedly chose not to take action.

So far, the allegations haven’t affected Huang’s future at Nvidia. He’ll continue to lead the company during the investigation. Trump has also stood by him since news broke of the affair, telling reporters this past weekend, “I think he’s an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it.”

Nvidia said it had “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised”

On Sunday, Huang wrote an internal memo to Nvidia employees — some of which are his own family members — writing, “I owe my family and all of you an apology. I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.” He then addressed Nvidia’s board, partners, and shareholders, adding that he is “fully committed” to leading the company and “will work to earn back your confidence.” This weekend, Nvidia’s board of directors launched an independent investigation into the national security allegations. Huang will not have control over the participants in the investigation, and Nvidia said it had “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised.”

Morningstar senior equity analyst Brian Colello wrote in a note on Monday that Morningstar “can’t rule out the investigation fading away and Nvidia returning to business as usual,” particularly since Huang is the “only CEO the company has ever known.” There’s also the fact that Huang’s Nvidia struck deals in August with six large financial institutions to provide more than $500 billion in financing for the buildout of AI data centers and other infrastructure, and as the US-China AI race intensifies, those are high-stakes agreements.

“It’s unclear to us what these partnerships and financing deals would look like with a different Nvidia CEO,” Colello wrote. “Costs of capital may rise for future investments if perhaps the most prominent CEO in the AI landscape is no longer orchestrating such deals.” Morningstar still maintained a very high uncertainty rating for the company, with Colello writing that it’s possible US approval of Nvidia to sell H20 chips to China will be revoked and that the company could ostensibly be sanctioned as well.

Trump told reporters on Sunday that Huang should remain CEO of Nvidia and that he would “certainly take him” on his next trip to China. “I haven’t heard he’s done anything wrong,” Trump told reporters. “He dealt with somebody, but you know, so does everybody else.”

#228. Why Nvidia CEO's affair with alleged Chinese spy matters

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has apologized for an affair with a woman the US believes was spying for China. The story could have profound implications for the world's most valuable company and the global AI race.

#227. Medium’s new TK app brings AI assistance directly into the writing process

Online publishing platform Medium on Monday launched a new app, called TK, which aims to bring AI technology deeper into the writing process. The idea behind TK is to make it easier to turn ideas into written words by incorporating an AI assistant into the editor itself, allowing users to ask for help by typing “@TK” followed by their request or prompt.

In publishing and journalism, the term “TK” serves as a placeholder used to mark where missing information can be added later. The “TK” stands for “to come,” but uses a “K” instead of “C” as there are few English words where the “tk” letter combination exists, making it stand out more easily in written text where it could be caught by proofreaders.

In Medium’s app, TK takes on a new role as it tries to help users fill in the blanks.

The company suggests the AI assistant could help answer questions that you might normally have to switch to another tab to quickly look up, like how many days are in a particular month. The app could also pull in bits of writing you had previously saved to the app when you were still fleshing out your ideas.

In addition, Medium says TK will ship with particular skills for editorial feedback, headline feedback, and others that could match your draft to the style guide for Medium publications.

Medium’s move to embrace AI in the early stages of writing could prove controversial at a time when the publishing and literary worlds have been reckoning with the role that artificial intelligence has played in the creation of new long-form prose. Multiple authors have lost book deals over suspicions that they used AI technology to help them write, while other literary awards have come under scrutiny after winners were suspected of writing with AI assistance.

While writers have long used automated tools for proofreading and light grammatical edits, there is growing concern across the publishing industry that an AI assistant’s editorial “suggestions” can become the writing itself, instead of being reflective of the thoughts in the writer’s own mind.

But because AI models were trained on human writing, there are also questions around the reliability of AI detection tools in terms of their ability to properly determine when something was written with AI, rather than with its assistance, versus not at all.

TK leans into this debate, taking the side that these nuances will ultimately no longer matter in the end. Instead, the company celebrates what AI can bring to the world of writing, saying that “artificial intelligence makes writing more important, not less,” and proclaiming that “AI is a technology that has the power to amplify human intelligence and it uses the written word as a foundation.”

Still, Medium concedes that AI does raise the possibility of low-quality content and blurring the line between what’s human-written versus AI-written. To address these concerns, TK allows authors to publish notes that transparently explain how AI was used in their writing.

“This is a start of what we believe is required to publish in the AI era, which is to make the provenance of writing clear, transparent, and inspectable, right down to the prompting and source material,” Medium explained in a blog post about the launch.

In addition to writing assistance, TK will help users remember and organize what they had previously written and suggest writing prompts. The app can also optionally connect to your own AI agent through TK’s MCP server or Claude skill, so the app can better understand your projects, your positions, and other ideas that could be stored in your agent’s memory.

Meanwhile, the new app incorporates human feedback in a social layer, the company says, allowing writers to share their early drafts with others and get feedback.

At launch, TK is available on the web, but a desktop app is on the roadmap, Medium says. The app can import and export Markdown, but it’s not a Markdown app itself. The writing in TK is also not encrypted, as the app is designed for collaboration, but users will be able to set access control permissions on their work.

Medium, which is profitable, says TK will be a free tool for those with a Medium account.

#226. David Ellison says AI could be the ‘biggest creative unlock in modern history’

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Paramount Skydance’s CEO compared AI to CGI, but said it ‘will never be a replacement’ for artists.﻿

Paramount Skydance’s CEO compared AI to CGI, but said it ‘will never be a replacement’ for artists.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

During Paramount Skydance’s latest earnings call, CEO David Ellison said “it’s hard to imagine” AI won’t have a bigger impact on the film industry than CGI. In response to a question about the company’s outlook for AI, Ellison said:

“I think when you zoom out and look at production, AI really has the potential to be the biggest creative unlock in modern history. The last time we saw anything close to this was really the, you know, advent of CGI and digital composites in the early 90s. That technology gave birth to amazing stories like Jurassic Park and Terminator 2, and it’s hard to imagine that AI is not going to have a more significant impact than that. And I personally believe that’s going to be an incredible unlock on creativity.”

Ellison gave Terminator as an example, describing how it cost $4 million to make, but “if you wanted to make that picture today, shot for shot, it’s $60 to 70 million.” He went on to suggest AI might be able to help with that: “We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budgets more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk.”

Ellison said AI will “significantly” reshape Paramount Skydance’s business, saying the company has been developing its own AI tools. As a result of those tools, Ellison claims, “content will be easier to produce, projects across the organization will speed up, and how we work will transform.” He emphasized that “all of this makes the value of premium IP and live events more valuable, not less.”

Ellison also noted that “in a world of AI slop, we think there will always be a premium for truly hand-crafted storytelling.” He added, “We also continue to believe that artificial intelligence is a tool for artists and will never be a replacement for them.”

#225. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#222. Anthropic joins AI’s natural gas frenzy, buys $3.4B worth of turbines

AI’s power crunch has become so acute that Anthropic, a company that has generally avoided buying physical infrastructure, is spending $3.4 billion on natural gas turbines.

The AI company, which is planning to go public this year, is buying Caterpillar generators that equipment supplier OnePWR had originally ordered, according to a Financial Times report. The generators are capable of producing up to 1.7 gigawatts of electricity.

Historically, Anthropic has preferred to rent rather than buy. The company disclosed in its S-1 filing in May that it is paying SpaceX subsidiary xAI $1.25 billion per month through May 2029 to use xAI’s computing power.

“We expect to enter into additional similar services contracts,” Anthropic stated in the filing, meaning more deals to rent computing power.

But as demand for compute ramps up, the market for power has also grown increasingly uncertain, which may have forced its hand. The backlog for natural gas turbines stretches into the early 2030s, and queues to connect to the electrical grid can be just as long.

Anthropic’s purchase looks like a last resort.

According to the report, OnePWR ordered the turbines a couple years ago but couldn’t find financing to complete the purchase. Anthropic had planned to buy them from OnePWR but instead went directly to Caterpillar, the manufacturer.

It’s not clear why OnePWR was unable to secure financing despite Anthropic’s apparent interest as a customer. Whatever the case, Anthropic likely wanted to keep the generating capacity to itself rather than give a competitor an opportunity to snap it up.

AI companies have been ramping up their purchases of natural gas equipment and supplies in recent years. Google, Amazon, Microsoft, and Meta have all announced plans to build massive natural gas power plants to power their data centers on-site, rather than drawing from the public grid.

The binge has given a second wind to fracking, the drilling method used to extract natural gas from underground rock. And if the buildout proceeds as planned, U.S. data centers alone could consume more natural gas than Germany and Japan combined by 2035. The reliance on natural gas also exposes hyperscalers to price fluctuations. One analyst expects that prices could more than triple by the end of the decade in some markets. If that happens, Anthropic might have a serious case of buyer’s remorse.

#218. Uber launches parking service following SpotHero acquisition, with an eye on robotaxis

Uber on Monday said it is launching a new service that lets customers reserve and pay for parking in major U.S. cities, about nine months after the ride-hailing company acquired parking app SpotHero.

The acquisition, announced back in February, gives Uber access to a network of 13,000 garages, lots, and valet services across more than 400 cities, the company said. And while Uber’s initial plans will focus on human drivers, the company also plans to leverage these parking spots to help the dozens of autonomous vehicle companies it has partnered with.

Uber said the parking spots could be used as staging and waiting locations for autonomous vehicles to help reduce congestion and deadheading, which refers to the miles a vehicle travels without a paying passenger or cargo on board.

For now, the company plans to focus on commuters driving their own vehicles. Uber said it will initially launch in 15 U.S. cities, including Atlanta, Austin, Boston, Chicago, Dallas, Denver, Los Angeles, Miami, Nashville, New York City, Philadelphia, San Diego, San Francisco, Seattle, and Washington, DC.

Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi indicated earlier this month that the company wants the app to be more relevant to commuters.

“Commute is one of our largest categories,” he said during Uber’s third-quarter earnings call. “And to be able to offer commuters an Uber to work, a pass to avoid surge, and parking if they want it when they want to drive is a great example of us building out our inventory of services to the commuter who is a top customer for us.”

The parking service is the latest in a series of efforts at Uber to build an ecosystem of products that keep customers engaged with its app for everything from hailing a ride and ordering food and groceries, to package delivery, renting cars, booking hotels, and connecting users with public transit and robotaxis.

Uber Parking can be accessed directly on the app’s homescreen. Users can enter their destination, select the date and time they need parking, and then review options on an interactive map. Users can filter the results to find self-parking, valet, garages, or surface lots. All pricing is shown upfront.

Once a user has found a parking spot, they can book after entering details about their vehicle, and use the payment method in their Uber wallet. Once they arrive at the parking spot, users will either scan a unique QR code or gain entry through license plate recognition. Uber said users will be able to cancel their bookings for free until the start time.

#216. Meta releases Muse Ember, a new lightweight model for agent execution

Meta Superintelligence Lab released a new AI model on Monday called Muse Ember, the lab’s lightest and most cost-efficient AI model yet. In its release, Meta said Ember was “designed for complex reasoning execution and the work agents repeat all day: tool calls, coding subtasks, routing, extraction, and long document parsing.”

Meta particularly emphasized Ember’s cost-per-task, using Artificial Analysis’s composite benchmark score to show Ember as cheaper and smarter than competitors like Anthropic’s Haiku and OpenAI’s Luna.

At 10 cents per million input tokens, Ember is significantly cheaper than frontier competitors like Haiku and Luna. Not only that, it’s cost-competitive with open source competitors like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and GLM 5.3 Flash.

Given the emphasis on low cost and accuracy at scale, Ember is designed to pair with a larger model like Meta’s Muse Wonder in an agentic harness. In the case of a customer-support agent, for instance, you might see a heavier model establishing rules for how to handle a particular issue, then sending a lighter-weight model to apply the rules across tens of thousands of cases. As Meta puts it in the announcement, “Muse Wonder plans, Muse Ember handles the volume.”

With volume-heavy use cases in mind, Meta also trained Ember to work well across different types of media, including text, images, and audio. The result makes the model surprisingly flexible in what kind of inputs it can handle. As Meta puts it in the announcement, “agents can read screenshots, charts, and scanned documents, or work from voice memos, meeting recordings, and video without a separate model.”

Ember is the fourth model released under Meta’s Muse brand, following the mid-weight Spark (released in April), Spark’s open source twin Glimmer (released in August), and the Wonder frontier model (released in October). The flagship consumer AI product, the Muse Agent, was released in September to unexpected success, with new features like Spaces and video chat rolling out earlier this month.

#215. Jensen Huang’s affair with alleged Chinese spy puts Nvidia’s China ambitions under scrutiny

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has never been quiet about his belief that the U.S. should continue selling AI chips to China, rather than shutting the country off from the technology. The revelation that he had an affair with an alleged Chinese spy threatens to complicate that argument.

The Financial Times reported Friday on Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer. Jensen Huang admitted to the affair and apologized in a memo to the company on Sunday. U.S. intelligence officials believe Alice Huang was recruited by Chinese intelligence to gather information about the CEO and influence the company’s efforts to loosen chip export restrictions.

Nvidia’s independent directors, working with outside legal counsel, are investigating the matter, and Congress is already demanding answers.

The controversy comes at a sensitive moment for Nvidia. Historically, China has been a big market for the company. It has shrunk in recent years due to strict U.S. export controls and an aggressive push by Beijing for domestic alternatives. In the three months ended July 26, Nvidia recorded $7.9 billion in revenue from customers headquartered in China, roughly 8% of total revenue.

Huang has repeatedly lobbied the U.S. government to expand access to China, and some of his persuasion tactics have been successful. Huang met repeatedly with President Donald Trump and administration officials last year; afterward, the government reversed restrictions on Nvidia’s H20 chips and allowed exports of its more powerful H200 processors.

On numerous occasions, Huang has publicly lamented export controls, calling them a “failure” at a conference last year and saying in May that it was “not wise” for the U.S. and China to create two distinct AI ecosystems. He has also warned that shutting Nvidia out of China’s compute market would enable the country to develop its own AI chips to challenge Nvidia internationally.

China is already moving in that direction. Reuters reported last week that about 600,000 H200 chips are being held in Hong Kong, awaiting shipment to mainland China. Beijing is pushing AI companies to prioritize buying chips from Chinese companies instead.

The idea that Huang needed any encouragement in the form of an attractive woman to push for greater access to China seems like a stretch, particularly because his lobbying efforts predate their reported meeting at an Nvidia event in early 2024. At the end of the day, Huang is a salesman trying to dominate one of the world’s biggest AI markets.

Still, the allegations involving Alice Huang open new scrutiny of the company’s business relationships and could taint any assurances Nvidia offers that it can prevent sensitive technology from reaching unauthorized customers.

Once made, rumors of questionable allegiances can be hard to shake. Secretary of State Marco Rubio reportedly warned officials close to Trump earlier this year about Huang’s relationship with an alleged spy. Even if Nvidia’s investigation finds that Huang didn’t share secrets with his paramour, the question is whether he’ll be able to convince Washington that Nvidia can still responsibly do business with China.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment.

#213. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.
